0 &= \phantom{-A\sqrt{5} +}\; B + \epsilon^3 F + \epsilon E + \epsilon^4 D + \epsilon^2 C,\\
0 &= \phantom{-A\sqrt{5} +}\; B + \epsilon^2 F + \epsilon^4 E + \epsilon D + \epsilon^3 C,
\end{align*}
which are the same with the equations of the original form; and similarly that
$ACDEFB$ means the system of three equations
\begin{align*}
0 &= -A\,\sqrt{5} + C + \phantom{\epsilon^3}D + \phantom{\epsilon}E + \phantom{\epsilon^4}F + \phantom{\epsilon^2}B,\\
0 &= \phantom{-A\sqrt{5} +}\; C + \epsilon^3 D + \epsilon E + \epsilon^4 F + \epsilon^2 B,\\
0 &= \phantom{-A\sqrt{5} +}\; C + \epsilon^2 D + \epsilon^4 E + \epsilon F + \epsilon^3 B,
\end{align*}
where the first equation is the same with the first equation, and the second and
third equations only differ by a factor from the second and third equations respectively
of the original form. And similarly as regards the others of the 10 forms.

I say that we have a second system $BCAF\bar{D}\bar{E}$ (where $\bar{D}$, $\bar{E}$ mean $-D$, $-E$
respectively): in fact, this denotes the system of three equations
\begin{align*}
0 &= -B\,\sqrt{5} + C + \phantom{\epsilon^3}A + \phantom{\epsilon}F - \phantom{\epsilon^4}D - \phantom{\epsilon^2}E,\\
0 &= \phantom{-B\sqrt{5} +}\; C + \epsilon^3 A + \epsilon F - \epsilon^4 D - \epsilon^2 E,\\
0 &= \phantom{-B\sqrt{5} +}\; C + \epsilon^2 A + \epsilon^4 F - \epsilon D - \epsilon^3 E,
\end{align*}
which are deducible from the original three equations.

We have, in fact, the identities
\begin{align*}
&- \sqrt{5}\; \{-B\sqrt{5} + \phantom{\epsilon^3}C + \phantom{\epsilon}A \phantom{+ \epsilon^4 F} + F - \phantom{\epsilon}D - \phantom{\epsilon^2}E\} \\
&= \phantom{+} 1\,(B \phantom{+ \epsilon^3 C} + C - A\sqrt{5} + \phantom{\epsilon^4}F + \phantom{\epsilon}D + \phantom{\epsilon^2}E) \\
&\quad + 2\,(B \phantom{+ \epsilon^3 C} + \epsilon^3 C \phantom{+ \epsilon F + \epsilon^4 D} + \epsilon^2 F + \epsilon D + \epsilon^4 E) \\
&\quad + 2\,(B \phantom{+ \epsilon^3 C} + \epsilon^2 C \phantom{+ \epsilon^4 F + \epsilon D} + \epsilon^3 F + \epsilon^4 D + \epsilon E),
\end{align*}
\begin{align*}
&(1 - \epsilon - \epsilon^3 + \epsilon^4)\, \{\phantom{-B\sqrt{5} +}\; C + \epsilon^3 A \phantom{+ \epsilon F} - \epsilon^4 D - \epsilon^2 E\} \\
&= \phantom{+} 1\,(B \phantom{+ \epsilon^3 C} + C - A\sqrt{5} + \phantom{\epsilon^4}F + \phantom{\epsilon}D + \phantom{\epsilon^2}E) \\
&\quad + (\epsilon + \epsilon^4)\,(B \phantom{+ \epsilon^3 C} + \epsilon^3 C \phantom{+ \epsilon F + \epsilon^4 D} + \epsilon^2 F + \epsilon D + \epsilon^4 E) \\
&\quad + (\epsilon^2 + \epsilon^3)\,(B \phantom{+ \epsilon^3 C} + \epsilon^2 C \phantom{+ \epsilon^4 F + \epsilon D} + \epsilon^3 F + \epsilon^4 D + \epsilon E),
\end{align*}
and
\begin{align*}
&(1 + \epsilon - \epsilon^2 - \epsilon^4)\, \{\phantom{-B\sqrt{5} +}\; C + \epsilon^2 A \phantom{+ \epsilon^4 F} - \epsilon D - \epsilon^3 E\} \\
&= \phantom{+} 1\,(B \phantom{+ \epsilon^3 C} + C - A\sqrt{5} + \phantom{\epsilon^4}F + \phantom{\epsilon}D + \phantom{\epsilon^2}E) \\
&\quad + (\epsilon^2 + \epsilon^3)\,(B \phantom{+ \epsilon^3 C} + \epsilon^3 C \phantom{+ \epsilon F + \epsilon^4 D} + \epsilon^2 F + \epsilon D + \epsilon^4 E) \\
&\quad + (\epsilon + \epsilon^4)\,(B \phantom{+ \epsilon^3 C} + \epsilon^2 C \phantom{+ \epsilon^4 F + \epsilon D} + \epsilon^3 F + \epsilon^4 D + \epsilon E),
\end{align*}
all which identities are at once verified on recollecting that
\[
1 = -\epsilon - \epsilon^2 - \epsilon^3 - \epsilon^4 \text{ and } \sqrt{5} = \epsilon - \epsilon^2 - \epsilon^3 + \epsilon^4.
\]

We can now write down the 6 systems, each of them under its 10 forms:
these, in fact, are [arranged in a table with 6 rows, the first three systems
being $ABCDEF$ and its 9 other forms, and systems 4--6 being analogous
forms with sign changes as indicated above].

The table shows the 60 forms arranged as follows:
\begin{itemize}
\item First system ($ABCDEF$): 10 forms --- cyclic permutations of $B,C,D,E,F$
and their reverses.
\item Second system ($BCAF\bar{D}\bar{E}$): 10 forms --- obtained from the first system
by the substitution which replaces $A \to B$, $B \to C$, $C \to A$, $D \to F$, $E \to \bar{D}$, $F \to \bar{E}$.
\item Third system ($CAB\bar{E}\bar{F}D$): 10 forms --- obtained by repeating
the substitution.
\item Fourth, fifth, and sixth systems: obtained by applying the substitutions
which change the upper line into the three forms of the first system
($ADEFBC$, $AEFBCD$, $AFBCDE$) to $BCAF\bar{D}\bar{E}$.
\end{itemize}

As to the formation of this table, observe that we have $ABCDEF$ and $BCAF\bar{D}\bar{E}$;
repeating the substitution, we have
\begin{align*}
&ABCDEF\\
&BCAF\bar{D}\bar{E}\\
&CAB\bar{E}\bar{F}D\\
&ABCDEF;
\end{align*}
viz.\ we have thus the $CAB\bar{E}\bar{F}D$ which belongs to the third system; but nothing
further, for the substitution is periodic of the third order, and the three forms are
merely repeated. But if, upon
\begin{align*}
&ABCDEF\\
&BCAF\bar{D}\bar{E},
\end{align*}
we operate successively with the substitutions which change the upper line into the
three forms of the first system
\[
ADEFBC, \quad AEFBCD, \quad AFBCDE,
\]
then the lower line is changed into the three forms
\[
DEAC\bar{F}\bar{B}, \quad EFAD\bar{B}\bar{C}, \quad FBAE\bar{C}\bar{D},
\]
which are forms belonging to the fourth, fifth, and sixth systems respectively.
By way of verification, observe that, for instance repeating upon the second line the
substitution $ABCDEF$, in place of $DEAC\bar{F}\bar{B}$, we obtain $C\bar{F}DAB\bar{E}$, which is one
of the forms of the third system, assumed to have been previously found; and so in
other instances.

Reverting to the three equations belonging to the form $ABCDEF$, by subtracting
the third from the second equation, we obtain a linear relation between the four
square roots $C$, $D$, $E$, $F$, viz.\ this is
\[
0 = (\epsilon^3 - \epsilon^2)\, (C - F) + (\epsilon - \epsilon^4)\, (D - E);
\]
and the same equation is obtained by means of the form $ABFEDC$. We thus obtain
from the thirty lines of either column of the preceding table, thirty such equations,
but obviously the number of such equations should be fifteen, for there can be but
one relation between any four square roots $C$, $D$, $E$, $F$; consequently each equation
will be obtained twice; and, in fact, it is clear that the forms $ABCDEF$ and
$BAF\bar{D}\bar{E}C$, and so in general any two forms which begin with the same pair of
letters, give the same equation. But for greater symmetry, I write down the thirty
equations in the order in which they are given by the left-hand column of the
table: the equations are [arranged in 6 groups of 5].

I write down the 15 equations in a different order, in some cases changing the
sign of the whole equation, as follows [the equations are arranged in 3 groups of 5]:
\begin{align*}
0 &= (\epsilon^3 - \epsilon^2)\, (A + C) + (\epsilon - \epsilon^4)\, (B + D),\\
0 &= (\epsilon^3 - \epsilon^2)\, (B + C) + (\epsilon - \epsilon^4)\, (A - E),\\
0 &= (\epsilon^3 - \epsilon^2)\, (A + B) + (\epsilon - \epsilon^4)\, (C + F),\\
0 &= (\epsilon^3 - \epsilon^2)\, (D + E) + (\epsilon - \epsilon^4)\, (A - B),\\
0 &= (\epsilon^3 - \epsilon^2)\, (B + F) + (\epsilon - \epsilon^4)\, (A - D),\\
0 &= (\epsilon^3 - \epsilon^2)\, (A + F) + (\epsilon - \epsilon^4)\, (B + E),\\
0 &= (\epsilon^3 - \epsilon^2)\, (A + D) + (\epsilon - \epsilon^4)\, (C + E),\\
0 &= (\epsilon^3 - \epsilon^2)\, (C + D) + (\epsilon - \epsilon^4)\, (A - F),\\
0 &= (\epsilon^3 - \epsilon^2)\, (E + F) + (\epsilon - \epsilon^4)\, (A - C),\\
0 &= (\epsilon^3 - \epsilon^2)\, (A + E) + (\epsilon - \epsilon^4)\, (D + F),\\
0 &= (\epsilon^3 - \epsilon^2)\, (B - E) + (\epsilon - \epsilon^4)\, (C - D),\\
0 &= (\epsilon^3 - \epsilon^2)\, (F - D) + (\epsilon - \epsilon^4)\, (B - C),\\
0 &= (\epsilon^3 - \epsilon^2)\, (E - C) + (\epsilon - \epsilon^4)\, (F - B),\\
0 &= (\epsilon^3 - \epsilon^2)\, (D - B) + (\epsilon - \epsilon^4)\, (E - F),\\
0 &= (\epsilon^3 - \epsilon^2)\, (C - F) + (\epsilon - \epsilon^4)\, (D - E).
\end{align*}

The first three of these equations, or writing $\lambda = \epsilon^3 - \epsilon^2$,
$\mu = \epsilon - \epsilon^4$, say
\begin{align*}
\lambda\,(A + C) + \mu\,(B + D) &= 0,\\
\lambda\,(B + C) + \mu\,(A - E) &= 0,\\
\lambda\,(A + B) + \mu\,(C + F) &= 0,
\end{align*}
constitute the entire system of independent linear relations between the square roots
$A$, $B$, $C$, $D$, $E$, $F$. The coefficients $\lambda$, $\mu$ are such that
\[
\lambda^2 - \mu^2 = \lambda\mu\, (= \epsilon - \epsilon^2 - \epsilon^3 + \epsilon^4,\; = \sqrt{5}),
\]
and it is hence easy to verify that the remaining twelve equations can be deduced
from these by the elimination of one or two of the square roots $A$, $B$, $C$. For
instance, to eliminate $A$ from the first and second equations, multiplying by $-\mu$, $\lambda$
and adding, we obtain
\[
(-\lambda\mu + \lambda^2)\, C + (-\mu^2 + \lambda^2)\, B - \mu^2 D - \lambda\mu E = 0,
\]
that is,
\[
\mu^2 C + \phantom{(-\mu^2 + \lambda^2)\,}\; \lambda\mu B - \mu^2 D - \lambda\mu E = 0,
\]
or finally
\[
\lambda\,(B - E) + \mu\,(C - D) = 0,
\]
which is one of the equations. And so again eliminating $A$ from the first and
third equations, we find
\[
\lambda\,(B - C) + \mu\,(C + F - B - D) = 0,
\]
that is,
\[
(\lambda - \mu)\,(B - C) + \mu\,(F - D) = 0,
\]
or multiplying by $\lambda$,
\[
\mu^2\,(B - C) + \lambda\mu\,(F - D) = 0,
\]
that is, finally
\[
\lambda\phantom{^2}\,(F - D) + \mu\,(B - C) = 0,
\]
which is one of the equations.

\begin{flushright}
\textit{Cambridge, 21 March 1887.}
\end{flushright}

\newpage
\section*{[868]}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{868. On the Intersection of Curves}
\begin{center}
\large\textbf{ON THE INTERSECTION OF CURVES.}
\end{center}
\markboth{ON THE INTERSECTION OF CURVES.}{ON THE INTERSECTION OF CURVES.}

\begin{flushright}
[From the \textit{Mathematische Annalen}, t.~xxx. (1887), pp. 85---90.]
\end{flushright}

It is only recently that I have studied Bacharach's paper ``Ueber den Cayley'schen
Schnittpunktsatz,'' \textit{Math.\ Ann.}, t.~xxvi. (1885), pp.~275---299: his theorem in regard
to the case where the $\delta$ points lie on a curve of the order $\gamma-3$ is a very
interesting and valuable one, but I consider it rather as an addition than as a
correction to my original theorem; and I cannot by any means agree that the
method by counting of constants is to be rejected as not trustworthy; on the
contrary, it seems to me to be the proper foundation of the whole theory; it must
of course be employed with due consideration of special cases. I reproduce the
theorem in what appears to me the complete form.

Writing with Bacharach
\[
r \equiv m, \quad r \equiv n, \quad r \equiv m + n - 3, \quad
\gamma = m + n - r, \quad \delta = \tfrac12(\gamma - 1)(\gamma - 2),
\]
and assuming $n \equiv m$, (these equations and inequalities are to be attended to throughout
the present paper), I consider two curves of the orders $m$, $n$ respectively meeting in
$\delta$ points $B$, and in $(mn - \delta)$ points $A$; and I state the theorem as follows:

$1^\circ$.\ The $mn - \delta$ points $A$ are in general such that a curve of the order $r$,
which passes through $mn - \delta - 1$ of these points, does not of necessity pass through
the remaining point; and in this case the general curve of the order $r$, which passes
through the $mn - \delta$ points $A$, has for its form of equation
\[
0 = L_{r-m} P_m + M_{r-n} Q_n,
\]
where $P_m = 0$, $Q_n = 0$ are the equations of the given curves and $L_{r-m}$, $M_{r-n}$ denote
functions of the orders $r-m$, $r-n$ respectively; and it thus appears that the curve
of the order $r$ through the $mn - \delta$ points $A$ passes also through the $\delta$ points $B$.

$2^\circ$.\ If however the $\delta$ points $B$ are on a curve of the order $\gamma - 3$, then the
$mn - \delta$ points $A$ are a system such that every curve of the order $r$ passing
through $mn - \delta - 1$ of these points passes through the remaining point; and in this
case the general curve of the order $r$, which passes through the $mn - \delta$ points $A$, has
for its form of equation
\[
0 = \Omega_r + L_{r-m} P_m + M_{r-n} Q_n,
\]
$\Omega_r = 0$ is a particular curve through the $mn - \delta$ points $A$, which does not go
through any of the points $B$; and consequently the curve of the order $r$ does not
pass through any of the points $B$.

For the proof of the theorem I premise as follows:

A curve of the order $r$ depends upon $\tfrac12 r(r+3)$ constants, or to use a convenient
expression, its Postulandum is $= \tfrac12 r(r+3)$: if the curve has to pass through a given
point, this imposes a single relation upon the constants, or say the Postulation is $= 1$;
similarly, if the curve has to pass through $k$ given points, this imposes $k$ relations,
or say the Postulation is $= k$. The points may be however a special system, for
instance, they may be such that every curve of the order $r$ which passes through
$k-1$ of the points, will pass through the remaining point; the Postulation is in this
case $= k-1$; and so in other cases. Assuming the Postulation of the $k$ points to
be $= k$, then the Postulandum of a curve of the order $r$ through the $k$ points is
$= \tfrac12 r(r+3) - k$. I stop to remark that the Postulation has reference to the particular
curve or other entity in question; thus in the case of a curve passing through
$k$ points, the Postulation for a curve of a certain order may be $= k$, and for a
curve of a different order it may be less than $k$.

Considering now, as above, two given curves of the orders $m$ and $n$ intersecting
in the $mn - \delta$ points $A$ and the $\delta$ points $B$, then assuming that the $mn - \delta$ points
are not a special system, viz.\ that their Postulation in regard to a curve of the
order $r$ is $= mn - \delta$, the Postulandum of a curve of the order $r$ through the $mn - \delta$
points is
\[
= \tfrac12 r(r+3) - mn + \delta,
\]
which is
\[
= \tfrac12(r-m+1)(r-m+2) + \tfrac12(r-n+1)(r-n+2) - 1,
\]
viz.\ this is identically true when for $\delta$ we write its value
\[
= \tfrac12(m+n-r-1)(m+n-r-2).
\]
But we have through the $mn - \delta$ points $A$, a curve
\[
L_{r-m} P_m + M_{r-n} Q_n = 0
\]
with the proper Postulandum: viz.\ $L_{r-m}$ contains $\tfrac12(r-m+1)(r-m+2)$ constants,
$M_{r-n}$ contains $\tfrac12(r-n+1)(r-n+2)$ constants, and there is a diminution $-1$ for the
constant which divides out; hence this is the general equation of the curve of the
order $r$ through the $mn - \delta$ points $A$; and the curve passes through the remaining
$\delta$ points $B$.

In the case where the $\delta$ points $B$ are on a curve of the order $\gamma-3$, (observe
that this is a single condition imposed on the $\delta$, $= \tfrac12(\gamma-1)(\gamma-2)$ points, for a curve
of the order $(\gamma-3)$ can be drawn through $\tfrac12\gamma(\gamma-3)$ points), it is to be shown that
the Postulation of the $mn - \delta$ points $A$ is $= mn - \delta - 1$; for, this being so, the
Postulandum of the curve of the order $r$ through the $(mn - \delta)$ points $A$ will be
\[
= \tfrac12 r(r+3) - mn + \delta + 1,
\]
and the equation of the curve will no longer be of the foregoing form, but it will
be of the form
\[
\Omega_r + L_{r-m} P_m + M_{r-n} Q_n = 0,
\]
$\Omega_r = 0$ being a particular curve through the $mn - \delta$ points $A$, which does not pass
through any of the points $B$. The proof depends on the theory of Residuation: which
for the present purpose may be presented under the following form.

Let $A$, $B$, \ldots\ denote systems of points upon a given Basis-curve, for instance
the foregoing curve $P_m = 0$, of the order $m$. And let $A = ci$ denote that the points
$A$ are the complete intersection of the basis-curve by some other curve; (this implies
that the number of the points is $= km$, a multiple of $m$, and the intersecting curve is
then of course a curve of the order $k$). It is clear that, if $A = ci$, and $B = ci$, then
also $A + B = ci$. But conversely we have the theorem that, if $A + B = ci$ and $A = ci$,
then also $B = ci$. And we at once deduce the further theorem: if $A + B = ci$, $B + C = ci$,
$C + D = ci$, then also $A + D = ci$. For the first and third relations give $A + B + C + D = ci$,
and the second relation then gives $A + D = ci$.

Starting now (see the diagram) with the $\delta$ points $B$ on a curve of the order
$\gamma - 3$, suppose that we have through these points the basis-curve $P_m = 0$ of the order
$m$, and another given curve $Q_n = 0$, of the order $n$; and let these besides meet in
the $mn - \delta$ points $A$. Let the curve of the order $\gamma - 3$ besides meet the basis-curve
in the $m(\gamma-3) - \delta$ points $C$; and through these let there be drawn a curve of the
order $m - 3$, which besides meets the basis-curve in the $m(r-n) + \delta$ points $D$. We
have here $A + B = ci$, $B + C = ci$, $C + D = ci$; consequently $A + D = ci$, that is, the
$mn - \delta$ points $A$ and the $m(r-n) + \delta$ points $D$ lie on a curve of the order $r$. The
curve of the order $m - 3$ passes through the $m(\gamma-3) - \delta$ points $C$; its Postulandum is thus
\[
= \tfrac12 m(m-3) - m(\gamma-3) + \delta,
\]
which is
\[
= \tfrac12(r-n+1)(r-n+2).
\]

In fact, substituting for $\gamma$, $\delta$ their values $= m + n - r$, and
$\tfrac12(m+n-r-1)(m+n-r-2)$
respectively, this equation is satisfied identically. The system of the $m(r-n) + \delta$ points
$D$ thus depends upon $\tfrac12(r-n+1)(r-n+2)$ constants, or say the Postulandum of
the points $D$ is $= \tfrac12(r-n+1)(r-n+2)$. It follows that the curve of the order $r$
through the $(mn - \delta)$ points $A$ and the $m(r-n) + \delta$ points $D$ \textit{cannot} have an equation
of the form
\[
L_{r-m} P_m + M_{r-n} Q_n = 0;
\]
for the intersections of this curve with the basis-curve $P_m = 0$ are given by the
equation $M_{r-n} Q_n = 0$, which contains only the
\[
\tfrac12(r-n+1)(r-n+2) - 1
\]
constants of $M_{r-n}$ (one constant of course divides out, giving the diminution $-1$).
The equation must have the more general form
\[
\Omega_r + L_{r-m} P_m + M_{r-n} Q_n = 0;
\]
and it thus appears that the Postulation of the $mn - \delta$ points $A$, instead of being
$= mn - \delta$, must be $= mn - \delta - 1$. This completes the proof.

I notice that, combining the last-mentioned identity
\[
\tfrac12 m(m-3) - m(\gamma-3) + \delta = \tfrac12(r-n+1)(r-n+2)
\]
with the like identity
\[
\tfrac12 n(n-3) - n(\gamma-3) + \delta = \tfrac12(r-m+1)(r-m+2),
\]
we obtain
\begin{align*}
&\tfrac12 m(m-3) + \tfrac12 n(n-3) - (m+n)(\gamma-3) + 2\delta\\
&= \tfrac12(r-n+1)(r-n+2) + \tfrac12(r-m+1)(r-m+2),
\end{align*}
and consequently, referring to a former result, the left-hand side should be
\[
= \tfrac12 r(r+3) - mn + \delta + 1;
\]
substituting for $\gamma$, $\delta$ their values, this is at once verified.

As appears by what precedes, Bacharach's special case is that in which the
$\delta$, $= \tfrac12(\gamma-1)(\gamma-2)$ points $B$ satisfy the single condition of lying on a curve of the
order $\gamma-3$. We may have between the points $B$ more than a single relation; in
particular, the points $B$ may be such as to include among themselves the complete
intersection of two curves of the orders $a$, $b$ respectively $(ab \equiv \delta)$: this will be the
case, if the given curves are of the form
\begin{align*}
P_m &= \lambda_a S_{m-a} - \mu_b R_{m-b},\\
Q_n &= \lambda_a V_{n-a} - \mu_b U_{n-b},
\end{align*}
it being understood, here and in what follows, that the values of $a$, $b$ are such that
the suffixes are none of them negative.

The two curves here intersect in the $ab$ points $(\lambda_a = 0,\; \mu_b = 0)$, and in the
$mn - ab$ points
\[
\begin{Vmatrix}
\,\lambda_a, & R_{m-b}, & U_{n-b}\, \\
\,\mu_b, & S_{m-a}, & V_{n-a}\,
\end{Vmatrix}
= 0,
\]
say the $(mn - \delta)$ points $A$ are $mn - ab - \Theta$ of the last-mentioned points, and the $\delta$
points $B$ are the remaining $\Theta$ points together with the $ab$ points. Here the general
form of the curve of the order $r$ passing through the $mn - ab$ points, and therefore
through the $mn - \delta$ points $A$, is
\[
\begin{Vmatrix}
L_{r-m-n+a+b}, & M_{r-n}, & N_{r-m} \\
\lambda_a\phantom{_{r-m-n+a+b}}, & R_{m-b}, & U_{n-b} \\
\mu_b\phantom{_{r-m-n+a+b}}, & S_{m-a}, & V_{n-a}
\end{Vmatrix}
= 0,
\]
where $L_{r-m-n+a+b}$, $M_{r-n}$, $N_{r-m}$ are arbitrary functions of the orders indicated by
the respective suffixes. The theory in regard to the number of constants is of course
altogether different from that which belongs to the case of the general functions
$P_m$, $Q_n$; and it is probable that much interesting theory would present itself in the
consideration of particular cases.

\begin{flushright}
\textit{Cambridge, 22 March 1887.}
\end{flushright}

\newpage
\section*{[869]}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{869. On the Transformation of Elliptic Functions}
\begin{center}
\large\textbf{ON THE TRANSFORMATION OF ELLIPTIC FUNCTIONS.}
\end{center}
\markboth{ON THE TRANSFORMATION OF ELLIPTIC FUNCTIONS.}{ON THE TRANSFORMATION OF ELLIPTIC FUNCTIONS.}

\begin{flushright}
[From the \textit{American Journal of Mathematics}, vol.~IX. (1887), pp. 193---224.]
\end{flushright}

\textsc{The} algebraical theory of the Transformation of Elliptic Functions was established
by Jacobi in a remarkably simple and elegant form, but it has not hitherto been
developed with much completeness or success. The cases $n = 3$ and $n = 5$ are worked
out very completely in the \textit{Fundamenta Nova} (1829); viz.\ considering the equation
\[
\frac{M\, dy}{\sqrt{1 - y^2 \cdot 1 - \lambda^2 y^2}} = \frac{dx}{\sqrt{1 - x^2 \cdot 1 - k^2 x^2}},
\]
($k = u^4$, $\lambda = v^4$; say this is the $Mk\lambda$- or $Muv$-form), Jacobi finds, in the two cases
respectively, a modular equation between the fourth roots $u$, $v$, say the $uv$-modular
equation, and, as rational functions of $u$, $v$, the value of $M$ and the values of the
coefficients of the several powers of $x$ in the numerator and denominator of the
fraction which gives the value of $y$; but there is no attempt at a like development
of the general case. I shall have occasion to speak of other researches by Jacobi,
Brioschi and myself; but I will first mention that my original idea in the present
memoir was to develop the following mode of treatment of the theory:

In place of the $Mk\lambda$-form, using the $\rho\alpha\beta$-form
\[
\frac{dy}{\sqrt{1 - 2\beta y^2 + y^4}} = \frac{\rho\, dx}{\sqrt{1 - 2\alpha x^2 + x^4}},
\]
(I write for greater convenience $2\alpha$, $2\beta$ in place of the $\alpha$ of Jacobi and Brioschi and
the $\beta$ of Brioschi), we can, by expanding each side in a series, integrating, and
reverting the resulting series for $y$, obtain $y$ in the form
\[
y = \rho x\, (1 + \Pi_1 x^2 + \Pi_2 x^4 + \ldots),
\]
where $\Pi_1$, $\Pi_2$, $\Pi_3$, \ldots\ denote given functions of $\rho$, $\alpha$, $\beta$. Taking $n$ odd and $= 2s + 1$
we assume for $y$ an expression
\[
y = \frac{x\, (A_s + A_{s-1} x^2 + \ldots + A_1 x^{2s-2} + x^{2s})}
{1 + A_1 x^2 + \ldots + A_{s-1} x^{2s-2} + A_s x^{2s}},
\]
where the last coefficient $A_s$ is at once seen to be $= \rho$. Comparing with the series-
value $y = \rho x\, (1 + \Pi_1 x^2 + \Pi_2 x^4 + \ldots)$, we have an infinite series of equations.
The first of these is, in fact, $A_s = \rho$; the next $(s-1)$ equations give linearly $A_1$, $A_2$, \ldots,
$A_{s-1}$ in terms of the coefficients $\Pi$; that is, of $\rho$, $\alpha$, $\beta$: the two which follow serve in
effect to determine $\rho$, $\beta$ as functions of $\alpha$: and then, $\rho$ and $\beta$ having these values,
all the remaining equations will be satisfied identically.

The process is an eminently practical one, so far as regards the determination
of the coefficients $A_1$, $A_2$, \ldots, $A_{s-1}$ as functions of $\rho$, $\alpha$, $\beta$; it is less so, and requires
eliminations more or less complicated, as regards the determination of the relations
between $\rho$, $\alpha$, $\beta$. As to this, it may be remarked that the problem is not so much
the determination of the equation between $\rho$ and $\alpha$ (or say the $\rho\alpha$-multiplier equation,
or simply the $\rho\alpha$-equation), and of the equation between $\beta$, $\alpha$ (or say the
$\alpha\beta$-modular equation, or simply the $\alpha\beta$-equation), as it is to determine the complete
system of relations between $\rho$, $\alpha$, $\beta$; treating these as coordinates, we have what may
be called the multiplier-modular-curve, or say the MM-curve, and the relations in
question are those which determine this curve.

In the absence of special exceptions, it follows from general principles that the
coefficients $A_1$, $A_2$, \ldots, $A_{s-1}$, \textit{qu\`{a}} rational functions of $\rho$, $\alpha$, $\beta$, must also be rational
functions of $\alpha$, $\beta$ or of $\alpha$, $\rho$; and I think it may be assumed that this is the case;
the method, however, affords but little assistance towards thus expressing them.

In connexion with the foregoing theory, I consider the solutions of the problem
of transformation given by Jacobi's partial differential equation (``Suite de Notices sur
les Fonctions elliptiques,'' \textit{Crelle}, t.~IV. (1829), pp.~185---193), and by what I call the
Jacobi-Brioschi differential equations. The first and third of these were obtained by
Jacobi in the memoir*, ``De functionibus ellipticis Commentatio,'' \textit{Crelle}, t.~IV. (1829),
pp.~371---390 (see p.~376); but the second equation, which completes the system, was,
I believe, first given by Brioschi in the second appendix to his translation of my
\textit{Elliptic Functions: Trattato elementare delle Funzioni ellittiche}: Milan, 1880. I had,
strangely enough, overlooked the great importance of these equations. I shall have
occasion also to refer to results, and further develop the theory contained in my
memoir, ``On the Transformation of Elliptic Functions,'' \textit{Phil.\ Trans.}, t.~CLXIV. (1874),
pp.~397---456, [578], and the addition thereto, \textit{Phil.\ Trans.}, t.~CLXXXIX. (1878), pp.
419---424, [692].

I remark that, while I have only worked out the formul\ae\ for the cases $n = 3$
and $n = 5$, and a few formul\ae\ for the case $n = 7$, the memoir is intended to be a
contribution to the general theory of the $\rho\alpha\beta$-transformation; I hope to be able to
complete the theory for the case $n = 7$.

\begin{flushright}
[* Ges.\ \textit{Werke}, bd.~I., pp.~295---318; in particular, p.~303.]
\end{flushright}

\newpage
\noindent\textbf{Art.\ Nos.\ 1 to 5.} \textit{The Fundamental Differential Equation.}

1.\ The transformation-equation is
\[
\frac{dy}{\sqrt{1 - 2\beta y^2 + y^4}} = \frac{\rho\, dx}{\sqrt{1 - 2\alpha x^2 + x^4}},
\]
where $n$ is the order of the transformation; viz.\ expanding each side in series,
integrating, and reverting, we obtain $y$ as a series $\rho x\, (1 + \Pi_1 x^2 + \Pi_2 x^4 + \ldots)$,
where $\Pi_1$, $\Pi_2$, \ldots\ are given functions of $\rho$, $\alpha$, $\beta$.

2.\ The $\rho\alpha\beta$-equation is connected with the $Muv$-equation by a quadric transformation.

Starting from the equation
\[
\frac{M\, dy}{\sqrt{1 - y^2 \cdot 1 - \lambda^2 y^2}} = \frac{dx}{\sqrt{1 - x^2 \cdot 1 - k^2 x^2}},
\]
we may, by effecting on each side a quadric transformation, convert this into
\[
\frac{dy}{\sqrt{1 - 2\, (2v^8 - 1)\, y^2 + y^4}}
= \frac{M^{-1}\, dx}{\sqrt{1 - 2\, (2u^8 - 1)\, x^2 + x^4}},
\]
and this being so, we have, between $M^{-1}$ and $2u^8 - 1$, the relation
\[
F\left(\frac{1}{M},\, 2u^8 - 1\right) = 0;
\]
or, conversely, if this be the form of the $Mu$-multiplier equation, then the $\rho\alpha$-multiplier
equation is $F(\rho,\, \alpha) = 0$.

3.\ The quadric transformations are
\[
x = \frac{\sqrt{1 - x^2}}{x\, \sqrt{1 - k^2 x^2}}, \quad
y = \frac{\sqrt{1 - y^2}}{y\, \sqrt{1 - \lambda^2 y^2}}.
\]

We have then only to show that
\[
\frac{dx}{\sqrt{1 - 2\, (2u^8 - 1)\, x^2 + x^4}}
= \frac{dx}{\sqrt{1 - x^2 \cdot 1 - k^2 x^2}};
\]
for then, in like manner,
\[
\frac{dy}{\sqrt{1 - 2\, (2v^8 - 1)\, y^2 + y^4}}
= \frac{dy}{\sqrt{1 - y^2 \cdot 1 - \lambda^2 y^2}},
\]
and we pass from the assumed differential relation between $x$, $y$ to the above-mentioned
differential equation between $x$, $y$.

4.\ For the quadric transformation between $x$, $x$, write
\[
\theta^{\frac12} = k - ik', \quad \theta^{-\frac12} = k + ik',
\]
(whence also $\theta = \dfrac{k - ik'}{k + ik'}$), and therefore
\[
\theta^{\frac12} + \theta^{-\frac12} = 2k, \quad
\theta + \theta^{-1} = 2k^2 - 2k'^2 = 2\, (2k^2 - 1), \; = 2\, (2u^8 - 1);
\]
we have
\begin{align*}
1 - \theta x^2 = 1 - \theta \, \frac{1 - x^2}{x^2\, (1 - k^2 x^2)}
&= \frac{1}{x^2\, (1 - k^2 x^2)}
\{-\theta + (\theta + 1)\, x^2 - k^2 x^4\},\\
&= \frac{-\theta}{x^2\, (1 - k^2 x^2)}\, (1 - \theta^{-1} k x^2)^2;
\end{align*}
and thence
\[
\sqrt{1 - \theta x^2} = \frac{\sqrt{-\theta}\, (1 - \theta^{-1} k x^2)}{x\, \sqrt{1 - k^2 x^2}},
\]
and in like manner
\[
\sqrt{1 - \theta^{-1} x^2} = \frac{\sqrt{-\theta^{-1}}\, (1 - \theta k x^2)}{x\, \sqrt{1 - k^2 x^2}}.
\]

But $\sqrt{-\theta} \cdot \sqrt{-\theta^{-1}} = 1$, and hence
\begin{align*}
\sqrt{1 - \theta x^2} \cdot \sqrt{1 - \theta^{-1} x^2}
&= \frac{(1 - \theta^{-1} k x^2)\, (1 - \theta k x^2)}{x^2\, (1 - k^2 x^2)}\\
&= \frac{1 - (\theta + \theta^{-1})\, k x^2 + k^2 x^4}{x^2\, (1 - k^2 x^2)};
\end{align*}
substituting for $\theta + \theta^{-1}$ its value $= 2\, (2u^8 - 1)$, the numerator is
\[
1 - 2\, (2u^8 - 1)\, k x^2 + k^2 x^4,
\]
and this divided by $(1 - k^2 x^2)$ is $= 1 - 2\, (2u^8 - 1)\, x^2 + x^4$;
hence
\[
\sqrt{1 - \theta x^2} \cdot \sqrt{1 - \theta^{-1} x^2}
= \frac{1 - 2\, (2u^8 - 1)\, x^2 + x^4}{x^2\, (1 - k^2 x^2)},
\]
and therefore
\[
\frac{dx}{\sqrt{1 - \theta x^2} \cdot \sqrt{1 - \theta^{-1} x^2}}
= \frac{x\, (1 - k^2 x^2)\, dx}{\sqrt{1 - 2\, (2u^8 - 1)\, x^2 + x^4}}.
\]

But we have
\[
x^2 = \frac{1 - x^2}{x^2\, (1 - k^2 x^2)},
\]
and thence, differentiating,
\[
2x\, dx
= \frac{-2x\, dx \cdot x^2\, (1 - k^2 x^2) - (1 - x^2)\, \{2x\, dx \cdot (1 - k^2 x^2) - 2k^2 x^3\, dx\}}
{x^4\, (1 - k^2 x^2)^2},
\]
\[
= \frac{-2x\, dx \cdot x^2\, (1 - k^2 x^2) - (1 - x^2) \cdot 2x\, dx \cdot (1 - 2k^2 x^2 + k^2 x^4)}
{x^4\, (1 - k^2 x^2)^2},
\]
which is
\begin{align*}
&= \frac{2x\, dx \{-x^2\, (1 - k^2 x^2) - (1 - x^2)\, (1 - k^2 x^2)^2\}}
{x^4\, (1 - k^2 x^2)^2}\\
&= \frac{2x\, dx \{-x^2 - (1 - x^2)\, (1 - k^2 x^2)\}}{x^4\, (1 - k^2 x^2)}\\
&= \frac{2x\, dx \{-1 + k^2 x^2 - k^2 x^4\}}{x^4\, (1 - k^2 x^2)}
= \frac{-2x\, dx \cdot (1 - k^2 x^4)}{x^4\, (1 - k^2 x^2)},
\end{align*}
that is,
\[
2x\, dx = \frac{-2x\, dx \cdot (1 - k^2 x^4)}{x^4\, (1 - k^2 x^2)},
\]
and thence
\[
x\, dx = \frac{-x\, dx \cdot (1 - k^2 x^4)}{x^4\, (1 - k^2 x^2)},
\quad \text{or} \quad x\, dx\, \{x^4\, (1 - k^2 x^2) + (1 - k^2 x^4)\} = 0,
\]
that is, $x\, dx \cdot (1 - k^2 x^2)\, (1 + x^4) = 0$, which is satisfied identically in virtue of the
original equation. Hence
\[
\frac{dx}{\sqrt{1 - 2\, (2u^8 - 1)\, x^2 + x^4}}
= \frac{dx}{\sqrt{1 - x^2 \cdot 1 - k^2 x^2}};
\]
and similarly as regards the corresponding equation in $y$, $\lambda$; hence the theorem is proved.

5.\ The theorem is the generalisation of a known result in the case of the first
order of transformation. In this case we have $x = y$, and the equation becomes
\[
\frac{dy}{\sqrt{1 - 2\beta y^2 + y^4}}
= \frac{\rho\, dy}{\sqrt{1 - 2\alpha y^2 + y^4}},
\]
where $\rho$ is at pleasure $= \pm 1$; so that the $\rho\alpha$-equation is $\rho^2 - 1 = 0$, and thence
$\alpha = \beta$. The relation between $\rho$ and $\alpha$ is that $\rho = 1$ corresponds to $\alpha = \beta$,
and $\rho = -1$ to $\alpha = -\beta$.

\newpage
\noindent\textbf{Art.\ Nos.\ 6 to 10.} \textit{The $\rho\alpha\beta$-equation; case $n=3$.}

6.\ The equation here is
\[
\frac{dy}{\sqrt{1 - 2\beta y^2 + y^4}}
= \frac{\rho\, dx}{\sqrt{1 - 2\alpha x^2 + x^4}};
\]
and assuming for $y$ an expression
\[
y = \frac{x\, (\rho + x^2)}{1 + \rho x^2},
\]
we find, comparing the coefficients of $x^2$ on the two sides,
\[
A_1 - \rho A_1 = \rho \Pi_1, \quad \text{viz. } A_{s-1} - \rho A_1 = \rho \Pi_1,
\]
or, substituting for $\Pi_1$ its value,
\[
A_{s-1} - \rho A_1 = \tfrac13(\alpha \rho - \beta \rho^3).
\]
Here $s = 1$, $A_1 = \rho$, and $A_{s-1} = A_0 = \rho$; and we have
\[
\rho - \rho \cdot \rho = \tfrac13(\alpha \rho - \beta \rho^3),
\]
that is,
\[
1 - \rho = \tfrac13(\alpha - \beta \rho^2),
\]
or say
\[
\beta = \frac{\alpha - 3 + 3\rho}{\rho^2}.
\]
Also, from the equation
\[
\frac{dy}{\sqrt{1 - 2\beta y^2 + y^4}}
= \frac{\rho\, dx}{\sqrt{1 - 2\alpha x^2 + x^4}},
\]
we have at once
\[
\rho^4 = \frac{1 - \beta}{1 - \alpha}, \quad \rho^4 = \frac{1 + \beta}{1 + \alpha},
\]
and from these, eliminating $\beta$, we find
\[
\rho^8 = \frac{1 - \beta^2}{1 - \alpha^2},
\]
which, combined with the equation
\[
\beta = \frac{\alpha - 3 + 3\rho}{\rho^2},
\]
gives
\[
\rho^8\, (1 - \alpha^2) = 1 - \frac{(\alpha - 3 + 3\rho)^2}{\rho^4},
\]
that is,
\[
\rho^{12}\, (1 - \alpha^2) = \rho^4 - (\alpha - 3 + 3\rho)^2,
\]
or say
\[
\rho^{12} - \rho^{12}\alpha^2 - \rho^4 + (\alpha - 3 + 3\rho)^2 = 0.
\]

7.\ Writing this in the form
\[
\rho^{12} - \rho^4 + 9(1 - \rho)^2 + 6\alpha(1 - \rho)\, (3 - 3\rho) + \alpha^2\, \{9(1 - \rho)^2 - \rho^{12}\} = 0,
\]
we have the terms without $\alpha$:
\[
\rho^{12} - \rho^4 + 9 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2 = 0,
\]
and the coefficient of $\alpha$:
\[
6\rho^{12} - 36\rho + 36 = 0,
\]
and the coefficient of $\alpha^2$:
\[
9 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2 - \rho^{12} = 0.
\]
We hence obtain
\[
\alpha = \frac{-3\rho^{12} + 18\rho - 18}{9 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2 - \rho^{12}},
\]
and substituting this value in the original equation, we find the $\rho\alpha$-equation; but
it is more convenient to write
\[
\alpha = -1 + \frac{9\rho^2 - 18\rho + 9}{9 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2 - \rho^{12}},
\]
and thence
\[
\alpha + 1 = \frac{9(1 - \rho)^2}{(3 - 3\rho - \rho^6)\, (3 - 3\rho + \rho^6)},
\]
and similarly
\[
\alpha - 1 = \frac{-2\rho^6 + 9\rho^2 - 18\rho + 8}{(3 - 3\rho - \rho^6)\, (3 - 3\rho + \rho^6)},
\]

8.\ From the equation $\beta = \dfrac{\alpha - 3 + 3\rho}{\rho^2}$, writing for $\alpha$ its value, we find
\[
\beta = \frac{-1}{\rho^2}\,
\frac{3\rho^{12} - 18\rho + 18 + 3\rho^2 - 9\rho + 9\rho^2 - \rho^{12}}{9 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2 - \rho^{12}},
\]
that is,
\[
\beta = \frac{-1}{\rho^2}\,
\frac{2\rho^{12} + 12\rho^2 - 27\rho + 27}{9 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2 - \rho^{12}},
\]
and we thence obtain
\[
\beta + 1 = \frac{-2\rho^{12} - 12\rho^2 + 27\rho - 27 + 9\rho^2 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2 - \rho^{14}}
{\rho^2\, (9 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2 - \rho^{12})},
\]
\[
= \frac{-2\rho^{12} + 6\rho^2 + 9\rho - 27 - \rho^{14}}
{\rho^2\, (9 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2 - \rho^{12})},
\]
and
\[
\beta - 1 = \frac{-2\rho^{12} - 12\rho^2 + 27\rho - 27 - 9\rho^2 + 18\rho - 9\rho^2 + \rho^{14}}
{\rho^2\, (9 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2 - \rho^{12})},
\]
\[
= \frac{-2\rho^{12} - 30\rho^2 + 45\rho - 27 + \rho^{14}}
{\rho^2\, (9 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2 - \rho^{12})}.
\]

9.\ The $\rho\alpha$-equation may be obtained by eliminating $\beta$ between the two equations
\[
\rho^4 = \frac{1 - \beta}{1 - \alpha}, \quad \rho^4 = \frac{1 + \beta}{1 + \alpha},
\]
which give
\[
\beta = \frac{\alpha - 3 + 3\rho}{\rho^2}, \quad \text{and} \quad
\rho^8 = \frac{1 - \beta^2}{1 - \alpha^2},
\]
and substituting for $\beta$ in the last equation. We find
\[
\rho^8\, (1 - \alpha^2) = 1 - \frac{(\alpha - 3 + 3\rho)^2}{\rho^4},
\]
that is,
\[
\rho^{12}\, (1 - \alpha^2) = \rho^4 - (\alpha - 3 + 3\rho)^2,
\]
which may be written
\[
\rho^{12} - \rho^4 + 9 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2 + \alpha\, (6\rho^{12} - 36\rho + 36)
+ \alpha^2\, (9 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2 - \rho^{12}) = 0,
\]
and the $\rho\alpha$-equation is thus
\[
\alpha^2\, (9 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2 - \rho^{12})
+ \alpha\, (6\rho^{12} - 36\rho + 36)
+ (\rho^{12} - \rho^4 + 9 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2) = 0.
\]

The discriminant of this equation is
\[
(6\rho^{12} - 36\rho + 36)^2
- 4\, (9 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2 - \rho^{12})\, (\rho^{12} - \rho^4 + 9 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2),
\]
which is, in fact, a perfect square; and the equation may be written
\[
\left\{\alpha\, (9 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2 - \rho^{12}) + (3\rho^{12} - 18\rho + 18)\right\}^2
= (3\rho^{12} - 18\rho + 18)^2
- (9 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2 - \rho^{12})\, (\rho^{12} - \rho^4 + 9 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2),
\]
where the right-hand side is
\[
= (3\rho^{12} - 18\rho + 18)^2 - (9 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2 - \rho^{12})\, (\rho^{12} - \rho^4 + 9 - 18\rho + 9\rho^2).
\]

10.\ For the case $n=3$, the modular equation in the $uv$-form is $u^4 + v^4 + 2uv\, (1 - u^2v^2) = 0$,
or say $(u^4 + v^4 + 2uv)^2 = 16u^4v^4$, that is $u^4 + v^4 + 2uv = \pm 4u^2v^2$, giving two equations;
these are, in fact, equivalent, the one going into the other by the change of sign of $u$ or $v$.

\newpage
\noindent\textbf{Art.\ Nos.\ 11 to 18.} \textit{The $\rho\alpha\beta$-equation; case $n=5$.}

11.\ The equation is
\[
\frac{dy}{\sqrt{1 - 2\beta y^2 + y^4}}
= \frac{\rho\, dx}{\sqrt{1 - 2\alpha x^2 + x^4}},
\]
where we assume
\[
y = \frac{x\, (\rho + A_1 x^2 + x^4)}{1 + A_1 x^2 + \rho x^4},
\]
where the coefficients satisfy $A_2 = \rho$, $A_1$ remaining to be determined.

12.\ The equations for the determination of $\rho$, $\beta$ as functions of $\alpha$ are obtained by
comparing the differential equation; the results are as follows. We have
\[
-S_1 \rho^3 + \rho^2 + R_1 \rho - 1 = 0,
\]
and
\[
(-S_2 + 3S_1{}^2)\, \rho^4 - (3R_1 S_1 + 1)\, \rho^2 + R_2 + 1 = 0,
\]
where $R_1$, $R_2$, $S_1$, $S_2$ are given symmetrical functions of the roots.

13.\ The verification that the equations are satisfied identically is obtained by
substituting the values of $R_1$, $R_2$, $S_1$, $S_2$ in terms of $\alpha$ and $\beta$; these are
\[
R_1 = \alpha, \quad R_2 = -\alpha^2 + 2\alpha + 1,
\]
and
\[
S_1 = \beta, \quad S_2 = -\beta^2 + 2\beta + 1.
\]

14.\ For the values of $\rho$ and $\beta$ in terms of $\alpha$, we have the two equations
\[
-S_1 \rho^3 + \rho^2 + R_1 \rho - 1 = 0,
\]
and
\[
(-S_2 + 3S_1{}^2)\, \rho^4 - (3R_1 S_1 + 1)\, \rho^2 + R_2 + 1 = 0,
\]
where the $R$'s and $S$'s are regarded as given functions of $\alpha$ and $\beta$ respectively.

15.\ It is to be observed that the equations are satisfied by $\rho^2 = 1$, $\alpha = \beta$; viz.\ we
have the transformation $y = \dfrac{x\, (\pm 1 + x^2)}{1 \pm x^2}$; that is, $y = \pm x$, which is the transformation
of the first order, $n = 1$. The two equations represent surfaces of the orders 4 and 6
respectively, and they have thus a complete intersection of the order 24. As part of
this, we have, as just shown, each of the two lines $(\rho = 1,\, \alpha = \beta)$ and $(\rho = -1,\, \alpha = \beta)$;
but there is a more considerable reduction of order to be accounted for, the proper
MM-curve being, as will appear, a unicursal curve of the order $= 6$.

16.\ Multiplying the second equation by $10\rho^2$, and for $10R_2$ and $10S_2$ writing their
values $27R_1{}^2 - 1$ and $27S_1{}^2 - 1$ respectively, we have
\[
(3S_1{}^2 + 1)\, \rho^6 - (30R_1 S_1 + 10)\, \rho^4 + (27R_1{}^2 + 9)\, \rho^2 = 0;
\]
and if herein we substitute for $S_1 \rho^3$ its value from the first equation, $= \rho^2 + R_1 \rho - 1$,
we have
\[
3\, (\rho^2 + R_1 \rho - 1)^2 + \rho^6 - 30R_1 \rho\, (\rho^2 + R_1 \rho - 1) - 10\rho^4 + (27R_1{}^2 + 9)\, \rho^2 = 0;
\]
that is,
\[
\rho^6 - 7\rho^4 - 24R_1 \rho^3 + 3\rho^2 + 24R_1 \rho + 3 = 0;
\]
viz.\ this is
\[
(\rho^2 - 1)\, (\rho^4 - 6\rho^2 - 24R_1 \rho - 3) = 0,
\]
containing, as it ought to do, the factor $\rho^2 - 1$. Throwing this out, and repeating the
first equation, we have
\[
\begin{aligned}
-\; S_1 \rho^3 + \rho^2 + \phantom{24}R_1 \rho - 1 &= 0,\\
\rho^4 \phantom{{} - 24R_1 \rho^3} - 6\rho^2 - 24R_1 \rho - 3 &= 0,
\end{aligned}
\]
which two equations may be replaced by
\[
\begin{aligned}
\rho^4 \phantom{{} - 24S_1 \rho^3} - 24S_1 \rho^3 + 18\rho^2 \phantom{{} - 27} - 27 &= 0,\\
\rho^4 \phantom{{} - 24S_1 \rho^3} - \phantom{24S_1} \rho^2 - 24R_1 \rho - \phantom{2} 3 &= 0.
\end{aligned}
\]

17.\ From these two equations we eliminate $R_1$ and obtain a single equation in $\rho$ and
$S_1$; and then, substituting for $S_1$ its value in terms of $\beta$, we obtain the equation in
$\rho$, $\beta$. Or, what is the same thing, eliminating $R_1$, we obtain the equation
\[
\rho^4 - 24S_1 \rho^3 + 18\rho^2 - 27 = 0,
\]
which, together with the equation
\[
-S_1 \rho^3 + \rho^2 + R_1 \rho - 1 = 0,
\]
constitutes the system of equations for the determination of $\rho$ and $\beta$ as functions of $\alpha$.

18.\ The two equations may be represented as follows: writing for shortness
$\Theta_1 = 0$ and $\Theta_2 = 0$ to denote the equations
\[
\Theta_1 = -S_1 \rho^3 + \rho^2 + R_1 \rho - 1,
\]
and
\[
\Theta_2 = (-S_2 + 3S_1{}^2)\, \rho^4 - (3R_1 S_1 + 1)\, \rho^2 + R_2 + 1,
\]
we have identically
\begin{align*}
\Theta_2 &= \rho^4 - 6\rho^2 - 24R_1 \rho - 3
- 10\rho^2\, (-S_1 \rho^3 + \rho^2 + R_1 \rho - 1)\\
&= \rho^4 - 6\rho^2 - 24R_1 \rho - 3 - 10\rho^2\, \Theta_1,
\end{align*}
and we thence have
\[
\Theta_2 + 10\rho^2\, \Theta_1 = \rho^4 - 6\rho^2 - 24R_1 \rho - 3.
\]

\newpage
\noindent\textbf{Art.\ Nos.\ 19 to 26.} \textit{The case $n = 5$ (continued); elimination of $R_1$; result for $\alpha\beta$-equation.}

19.\ The elimination of $R_1$ from the two equations
\[
-S_1 \rho^3 + \rho^2 + R_1 \rho - 1 = 0, \quad \text{and} \quad
\rho^4 - 6\rho^2 - 24R_1 \rho - 3 = 0,
\]
gives the equation
\[
\rho^4 - 24S_1 \rho^3 + 18\rho^2 - 27 = 0,
\]
and this being so, we have
\[
R_1 = \frac{S_1 \rho^3 - \rho^2 + 1}{\rho},
\]
and substituting in the second equation, we have identically
\[
\rho^4 - 6\rho^2 - 24\, (S_1 \rho^3 - \rho^2 + 1) - 3 = 0,
\]
that is,
\[
\rho^4 - 24S_1 \rho^3 + 18\rho^2 - 27 = 0,
\]
as above.

20.\ To complete the theory for the case $n = 5$, we require the equation between $\alpha$
and $\beta$ (the $\alpha\beta$-equation) and the expression for $\rho$ as a rational function of $\alpha$.
The $\alpha\beta$-equation is obtained by eliminating $\rho$ from the two equations; and the value
of $\rho$ is then to be obtained from the resulting equation. The process is somewhat
complicated, but the final result is as follows: the $\alpha\beta$-equation is
\[
\alpha^3 - \beta^3 - \alpha\beta\, (\alpha\beta - 2)\, (\alpha - \beta) = 0,
\]
or say
\[
(\alpha - \beta)\, (\alpha^2 + \alpha\beta + \beta^2) - \alpha\beta\, (\alpha\beta - 2)\, (\alpha - \beta) = 0,
\]
that is,
\[
(\alpha - \beta)\, \{\alpha^2 + \alpha\beta + \beta^2 - \alpha\beta\, (\alpha\beta - 2)\} = 0,
\]
and throwing out the factor $\alpha - \beta$, which corresponds to the first order transformation,
we have
\[
\alpha^2 + \alpha\beta + \beta^2 - \alpha\beta\, (\alpha\beta - 2) = 0,
\]
that is,
\[
\alpha^2 + \alpha\beta + \beta^2 - \alpha^2\beta^2 + 2\alpha\beta = 0,
\]
or
\[
\alpha^2 + 3\alpha\beta + \beta^2 - \alpha^2\beta^2 = 0,
\]
which is the required $\alpha\beta$-modular equation.

21.\ The equation may also be written in the form
\[
(\alpha^2 - 1)\, (\beta^2 - 1) - (\alpha\beta + \alpha + \beta)\, (\alpha\beta - \alpha - \beta) = 0,
\]
or
\[
(\alpha^2 - 1)\, (\beta^2 - 1) = (\alpha\beta + \alpha + \beta)\, (\alpha\beta - \alpha - \beta).
\]

22.\ To obtain the value of $\rho$, we observe that the two equations giving $\rho$ in terms
of $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are
\[
-S_1 \rho^3 + \rho^2 + R_1 \rho - 1 = 0,
\]
and
\[
\rho^4 - 6\rho^2 - 24R_1 \rho - 3 = 0.
\]
Eliminating $R_1$, we have the equation in $\rho$ and $\beta$; and then, by means of the
$\alpha\beta$-equation, we can express $\beta$ in terms of $\alpha$, and so obtain $\rho$ as a function of $\alpha$.

23.\ We have, in fact,
\[
R_1 = \alpha, \quad S_1 = \frac{\rho^2 + \alpha\rho - 1}{\rho^3},
\]
and substituting these values in the equation
\[
\rho^4 - 24S_1 \rho^3 + 18\rho^2 - 27 = 0,
\]
we find
\[
\rho^4 - 24\, (\rho^2 + \alpha\rho - 1) + 18\rho^2 - 27 = 0,
\]
that is,
\[
\rho^4 - 6\rho^2 - 24\alpha\rho - 3 = 0,
\]
which is the $\rho\alpha$-equation.

24.\ The equation is, in fact, a quartic in $\rho$; and the value of $\rho$ can be expressed in
terms of $\alpha$ by means of the well-known formul\ae\ for the solution of a quartic equation.
It is to be observed, however, that the equation is reducible; in fact, it may be written
\[
(\rho^2 + 2\rho - 1)^2 - 4\alpha\rho\, (\rho^2 + 2\rho - 1) + 4\rho^2\, (\alpha^2 - 1) = 0,
\]
and hence we have
\[
\rho^2 + 2\rho - 1 = 2\rho\, (\alpha \pm \sqrt{\alpha^2 - 1}),
\]
which gives
\[
\rho = -1 + \alpha \pm \sqrt{\alpha^2 - 1} \pm \sqrt{(-1 + \alpha \pm \sqrt{\alpha^2 - 1})^2 + 1}.
\]

25.\ The $\alpha\beta$-equation may also be obtained from the modular equation in the $uv$-form.
The modular equation for $n = 5$ is
\[
u^6 - u^6 + 5u^2v^2\, (u^2 - v^2) + 4uv\, (1 - u^4v^4) = 0,
\]
and this, by means of the relations
\[
\alpha = \frac{1 + u^8}{6u^4}, \quad \beta = \frac{1 + v^8}{6v^4},
\]
gives the $\alpha\beta$-equation as above.

26.\ The expression for the multiplier $M$ in terms of $u$ and $v$ is
\[
M = \frac{v\, (1 - uv^3)}{v - u^5},
\]
and the corresponding expression for $\rho$ in terms of $\alpha$ is obtained from this by means
of the quadric transformation.

\newpage
\noindent\textbf{Art.\ Nos.\ 27 to 32.} \textit{The case $n = 7$.}

27.\ For the case $n = 7$, we have $s = 3$, and the assumed form is
\[
y = \frac{x\, (\rho + A_2 x^2 + A_1 x^4 + x^6)}{1 + A_1 x^2 + A_2 x^4 + \rho x^6}.
\]

28.\ The first three equations are
\begin{align*}
A_2 - \rho A_2 &= \rho \Pi_1, \quad \text{viz. } A_{s-1} - \rho A_1 = \rho \Pi_1,\\
A_1 - A_2 A_1 + \rho A_2 &= \rho \Pi_2, \\
1 - A_1{}^2 + A_2 A_1 + \rho A_2 &= \rho \Pi_3.
\end{align*}

29.\ From these, eliminating $A_1$ and $A_2$, we obtain two equations between $\rho$, $\alpha$, $\beta$;
these are of the forms
\[
\Theta_1 = 0, \quad \Theta_2 = 0,
\]
where $\Theta_1 = 0$ is the equation
\[
-S_1 \rho^3 + \rho^2 + R_1 \rho - 1 = 0,
\]
and $\Theta_2 = 0$ is the equation
\[
(-S_2 + 3S_1{}^2)\, \rho^4 - (3R_1 S_1 + 1)\, \rho^2 + R_2 + 1 = 0.
\]

30.\ These equations serve to determine $\rho$ and $\beta$ as functions of $\alpha$; the process is
analogous to that for the case $n = 5$, but the details are more complicated. The resulting
$\alpha\beta$-modular equation is
\[
\rho^8 - 28\rho^6 - 112\alpha \rho^5 - 210\rho^4 - 224\alpha \rho^3
+ (-1484 + 1344\alpha^2)\, \rho^2 + (-560\alpha + 512\alpha^3)\, \rho + 7 = 0.
\]

31.\ The foregoing equation is the $\rho\alpha$-equation for the case $n = 7$. The corresponding
$\alpha\beta$-modular equation has been obtained in a different form by Prof.\ Klein; it is
expressible in terms of the absolute invariants of the quartic curves, and it presents
itself in a very elegant form in his theory.

32.\ The equations should lead to the before-mentioned $\alpha\beta$-modular equation
and to expressions for $A_1$, $A_2$ as rational functions of $\alpha$, $\rho$; and they should be, all
five of them, satisfied by these results; but I do not see how the results are to be
worked out; there is, so far as appears, no clue to the discovery of the rational
functions of $\alpha$, $\rho$.

\newpage
\noindent\textbf{Art.\ Nos.\ 33.} \textit{The General Transformation, $n = 2s + 1$.}

33.\ The equation here is
\[
\frac{\rho + A_{s-1} x^2 + \ldots}{1 + A_1 x^2 + \ldots} = \rho\, (1 + \Pi_1 x^2 + \ldots).
\]
The general theory is sufficiently illustrated by the preceding particular cases, and I
wish at present only to notice the equation obtained by comparing the coefficients of
$x^2$; viz.\ this is $A_{s-1} - \rho A_1 = \rho \Pi_1$, or, substituting for $\Pi_1$ its value,
\[
A_{s-1} - \rho A_1 = \tfrac13(\alpha \rho - \beta \rho^3).
\]

\newpage
\noindent\textbf{Art.\ Nos.\ 34 to 42.} \textit{The Jacobi-Brioschi Equations.}

34.\ These were obtained for the differential equation
\[
\frac{dx}{\sqrt{a'x^4 + b'x^3 + c'x^2 + d'x + e'}}
= \frac{dy}{\sqrt{ay^4 + by^3 + cy^2 + dy + e}};
\]
viz.\ if this be satisfied by $y = U \div V$, where $U$, $V$ are rational and integral functions
of $x$ of the degrees $n$ and $n-1$ respectively, then, writing for shortness
\[
\phi = a'x^4 + b'x^3 + c'x^2 + d'x + e',
\]
and using accents to denote differentiation in regard to $x$, the numerator and denom-
inator $U$, $V$ satisfy the equations
\begin{align*}
(VV'' - V'^2)\, \phi + \tfrac12 VV' \cdot \phi' \phantom{{} + aU^2 \;}
&+ \phantom{(c-{}} aU^2 + \phantom{{}\tfrac12 dUV +{}} \tfrac12 bUV + pV^2 = 0,\\
-(VU'' + V''U - 2V'U')\, \phi - \tfrac12(VU' + V'U)\, \phi'
&+ \tfrac12 bU^2 + (c - 2p)\, UV + \tfrac12 dV^2 = 0,\\
(UU'' - U'^2)\, \phi + \tfrac12 UU' \cdot \phi' \phantom{{} + pU^2 \;}
&+ \phantom{(c-{}} pU^2 + \phantom{{}\tfrac12 dUV +{}} \tfrac12 dUV + \phantom{{}c\,} eV^2 = 0,
\end{align*}
where $p$ is a function $= ax^2 + bx + c$, with coefficients $a$, $b$, $c$, the values of which have
to be determined. By way of verification, observe that, multiplying by $U^2$, $UV$, $V^2$,
and adding, we obtain
\[
-(VU' - V'U)^2 \phi + aU^4 + bU^3V + cU^2V^2 + dUV^3 + eV^4 = 0;
\]
that is,
\[
-\frac{1}{V^2}(VU' - V'U)^2\, (a'x^4 + b'x^3 + c'x^2 + d'x + e')
+ ay^4 + by^3 + cy^2 + dy + e = 0,
\]
the result obtained by substituting for $y$ its value, $= U \div V$, in the differential equation.

35.\ Considering the foregoing special form
\[
\frac{dx}{\sqrt{1 - 2\alpha x^2 + x^4}}
= \frac{dy}{\rho\, \sqrt{1 - 2\beta y^2 + y^4}},
\]
so that $a$, $b$, $c$, $d$, $e$ have the values $\rho^2$, $0$, $-2\beta\rho^2$, $0$, $\rho^2$ and $\phi$ is $= 1 - 2\alpha x^2 + x^4$, the
equations are
\begin{align*}
(VV'' - V'^2)\, \phi + \phantom{{}\tfrac12(VU'+V'U)\,} \tfrac12 VV' \cdot \phi'
&+ \rho^2 U^2 + \phantom{(2\beta\rho^2+{}} pV^2 = 0,\\
-(VU'' + V''U - 2V'U')\, \phi + \tfrac12(VU' + V'U)\, \phi'
&- (2\beta\rho^2 + 2p)\, UV \phantom{{}+ \rho^2V^2} = 0,\\
(UU'' - U'^2)\, \phi + \phantom{{}\tfrac12(VU'+V'U)\,} \tfrac12 UU' \cdot \phi'
&+ \phantom{(2\beta\rho^2+{}} pU^2 + \phantom{{}\rho^2V^2} \rho^2 V^2 = 0.
\end{align*}

36.\ Writing $U = x\, (A_s + A_{s-1} x^2 + \ldots + x^{2s})$, $V = 1 + A_1 x^2 + \ldots + A_s x^{2s}$,
and observing that $\phi = 1 - 2\alpha x^2 + x^4$, $\phi' = -4\alpha x + 4x^3$, the equations become
\begin{align*}
\Theta_s &= 0,\\
\Theta_{s-1} &= 0,\\
&\vdots\\
\Theta_0 &= 0,\\
\Theta_{-1} &= 0;
\end{align*}
a system of $2s + 2$, $= n + 1$ equations, which are satisfied by proper values of the
$s + 1$, $= \tfrac12(n + 1)$ coefficients $A$, and the function $p$ of the second degree; that is, they
are satisfied by $\tfrac12(n + 1) + 3$, $= \tfrac12(n + 7)$ disposable constants; and the number of equations
being $= n + 1$, there remain $\tfrac12(n + 7) - (n + 1)$, $= \tfrac12(5 - n)$ constants disposable.

37.\ This is $= 1$ for $n = 3$, $= 0$ for $n = 5$, and negative for $n > 5$. We have thus
for $n = 3$ a single disposable constant; for $n = 5$ the number of constants is just
sufficient; and for $n > 5$ the equations are over-determined.

38.\ For the case $n = 3$, we have $s = 1$, and the equations are
\begin{align*}
\Theta_1 &= 0,\\
\Theta_0 &= 0,\\
\Theta_{-1} &= 0;
\end{align*}
three equations for the determination of $A_1$, that is, $\rho$, and the function $p$, which
contains three disposable constants; we have thus four unknowns and three equations,
leaving one constant disposable, as above.

39.\ For the case $n = 5$, we have $s = 2$, and the equations are
\begin{align*}
\Theta_2 &= 0,\\
\Theta_1 &= 0,\\
\Theta_0 &= 0,\\
\Theta_{-1} &= 0;
\end{align*}
four equations for the determination of $A_1$, $A_2$, that is, $A_1$, $\rho$, and the function $p$,
which contains three disposable constants; we have thus five unknowns and four equations,
leaving no constant disposable, as above.

40.\ For the case $n = 7$, we have $s = 3$, and the equations are
\begin{align*}
\Theta_3 &= 0,\\
\Theta_2 &= 0,\\
\Theta_1 &= 0,\\
\Theta_0 &= 0,\\
\Theta_{-1} &= 0;
\end{align*}
five equations for the determination of $A_1$, $A_2$, $A_3$, that is, $A_1$, $A_2$, $\rho$, and the
function $p$, which contains three disposable constants; we have thus six unknowns and
five equations, but the number of disposable constants is $-1$, that is, the equations are
over-determined.

41.\ It is to be observed that the equations are not all independent; in fact, there
is an identical relation between them, so that the number of independent equations is
less than the number of equations by one. This may be seen as follows: the equations
were obtained from the consideration that the differential equation
\[
\frac{dx}{\sqrt{1 - 2\alpha x^2 + x^4}}
= \frac{dy}{\rho\, \sqrt{1 - 2\beta y^2 + y^4}}
\]
is satisfied by $y = U \div V$; and this condition gives rise to the identity
\[
-(VU' - V'U)^2\, (1 - 2\alpha x^2 + x^4)
+ \rho^2\, (U^4 - 2\beta U^2 V^2 + V^4) = 0,
\]
which is an identity in $x$, and therefore gives rise to $2n + 1$ relations between the
coefficients; but these are not all independent, for they must satisfy the condition that
the equation $y = U \div V$ is a solution of the differential equation; and this condition
imposes one relation upon the coefficients, so that the number of independent relations
is $2n$, and the number of independent equations is $n + 1$, as above.

42.\ I consider the case $n = 7$, in order to show the form of the equations which
have to be solved; these equations are
\begin{align*}
\makebox[3em][r]{$2A_1$} - 2A_1 \cdot 1 &= 0,\\
\makebox[3em][r]{$12A_2$} - 2A_1{}^2 - 2A_1 \cdot 2A_1 - 7 \cdot 1
- 2\alpha\, (2A_1 + 1 \cdot 2A_1) + \rho^2 \cdot \rho^2 &= 0,\\
\makebox[3em][r]{$30\rho$} - 2A_1 A_2 + 2A_1 - 2A_1\, (2A_2 + A_1{}^2) - 5 \cdot 2A_1 \\
&\qquad\qquad - 2\alpha\, (12A_2 - 2A_1{}^2 + 2\, (2A_2 + A_1{}^2)) + \rho^2 \cdot 2\rho A_2 = 0,\\
\makebox[3em][r]{$8A_1\rho$} - 4A_2{}^2 + 12A_2 - 2A_1{}^2 - 2A_1\, (2\rho + 2A_1 A_2) - 3\, (2A_2 + A_1{}^2) \\
&\qquad\qquad - 2\alpha\, (30\rho - 2A_1 A_2 + 3\, (2\rho + 2A_1 A_2)) + \rho^2\, (2\rho A_1 + A_2{}^2) = 0,\\
-6A_2\rho + 30\rho - 2A_1 A_2 - 2A_1\, (2A_1\rho + A_2{}^2) - 1\, (2\rho + 2A_1 A_2) \\
&\qquad\qquad - 2\alpha\, (8A_1\rho - 4A_2{}^2 + 4\, (2A_1\rho + A_2{}^2)) + \rho^2\, (2\rho + 2A_1 A_2) = 0,\\
-6\rho^2 \;\; + 8A_1\rho - 4A_2{}^2 - 2A_1\, (2A_2\rho) + 1\, (2A_1\rho + A_2{}^2) \\
&\qquad\qquad - 2\alpha\, (-6A_2\rho + 5\, (2A_2\rho)) + \rho^2\, (2A_2 + A_1{}^2) = 0,\\
\makebox[3em][r]{$0$} \quad\;\; - 6A_2\rho - 2A_1 \cdot \rho^2 + 3\, (2A_2\rho)
- 2\alpha\, (-6\rho^2 + 6 \cdot \rho^2) + \rho^2 \cdot 2A_1 &= 0,\\
\makebox[3em][r]{$0$} \quad\;\; - 6\rho^2 + 5 \cdot \rho^2 - 2\alpha\, (0 + 7 \cdot 0) + \rho^2 \cdot 1 &= 0;
\end{align*}
viz.\ the first, seventh and eighth equations are satisfied identically, and there remain
five equations connecting $\rho$, $\alpha$, $A_1$, $A_2$.

\newpage
\noindent\textbf{Art.\ Nos.\ 43 to 48.} \textit{The Jacobi Partial Differential Equation.}

43.\ Writing, as above, $2\alpha$ in place of Jacobi's $\alpha$, this is
\[
(1 - 2\alpha x^2 + x^4)\, \frac{d^2 z}{dx^2}
+ (n - 1)\, (2\alpha x - 2x^3)\, \frac{dz}{dx}
+ n\, (n - 1)\, x^2 z - 4n\, (\alpha^2 - 1)\, \frac{dz}{d\alpha} = 0,
\]
satisfied by the numerator and denominator $U$, $V$, each of them taken with the
same proper value of the coefficient $A_0$, or, what is the same thing, by the values
\begin{align*}
U &= \Theta x\, (A_s + A_{s-1} x^2 + A_{s-2} x^4 + \ldots + A_1 x^{2s-2} + x^{2s}),\\
V &= \Theta \phantom{x}\; (1 + A_1 x^2 + A_2 x^4 + \ldots + A_{s-1} x^{2s-2} + A_s x^{2s}),
\end{align*}
where $\Theta$ is an arbitrary constant multiplier.

44.\ Writing for shortness
\[
\phi = 1 - 2\alpha x^2 + x^4, \quad \psi = 2\alpha x - 2x^3,
\]
the equation becomes
\[
\phi \, \frac{d^2 z}{dx^2} + (n - 1)\, \psi \, \frac{dz}{dx} + n\, (n - 1)\, x^2 z - 4n\, (\alpha^2 - 1)\, \frac{dz}{d\alpha} = 0.
\]

45.\ Assuming $z = a_0 + a_1 x + a_2 x^2 + \ldots$, the equation gives a relation between the
coefficients $a_0$, $a_1$, $a_2$, \ldots\ and their derivatives in regard to $\alpha$. In particular, for $z = U$
and $z = V$, we obtain the coefficients $A_1$, $A_2$, \ldots, $A_s$ as functions of $\alpha$.

46.\ For the case $n = 3$, the equation gives at once the value of $A_1$, and thence
the values of $\rho$ and $\beta$ as functions of $\alpha$. For the case $n = 5$, the equation gives
two relations between $A_1$ and $\alpha$, from which $A_1$ and $\rho$ can be determined.

47.\ For the case $n = 7$, the equation gives three relations between $A_1$, $A_2$ and $\alpha$;
but these are not sufficient for the complete determination of the coefficients, and the
process becomes more complicated.

48.\ The Jacobi partial differential equation affords a direct method of obtaining the
transformation-formul\ae\; but the process is, in general, one of great difficulty, and the
results are not obtained in the most convenient form. The method of the $\rho\alpha\beta$-equation
is, in many respects, preferable; but the two methods are essentially connected, and
the results obtained by the one method may always be verified by the other.

\begin{flushright}
\textit{Cambridge, 1887.}
\end{flushright}
\clearpage

% PAPER 870

\section[870]{870. On the Transformation of Elliptic Functions (Sequel).}
\label{sec:870}

[From the \textit{Proceedings of the London Mathematical Society}, vol.\ xix.\ (1888), pp.\ 425--478.]

\bigskip\noindent
79.\ It will presently appear that $p$ is, in fact, expressible as a rational function of $p$, $a$: but I am unable to obtain this expression in a simple form. Admitting that $p$ is thus expressible, a direct process for obtaining the expression is as follows. Writing
\[
k = \frac{8p\,(p^{2}+7)\,a + p^{4} + 29p^{2} + 49}{p^{4}+5p^{2}+1},
\]
and by means hereof introducing in place of $a$ into the equation $p$, we have for $k$ a cubic equation,
\[
ak^{3} + bk^{2} + ck + d = 0,
\]
where the coefficients $a$, $b$, $c$, $d$ are given rational functions of $p$.

This equation may be written
\[
a\left(k + \frac{b}{2a}\right)^{2} + \left(c - \frac{b^{2}}{4a}\right)k + \left(d - \frac{b^{2}}{4a}\right) = 0,
\]
where $b = b - 2a\xi$, $c = c - a\xi^{2}$; and the last three terms will be a square if only $c^{2} - 4bd = 0$; that is, if
\[
(2a\xi - b)^{2} + 4a\,(2a\xi - c) = 0,
\]
a biquadratic equation in $\xi$ which ($p$ being expressible as above) must have one of its roots $= \text{a rational function of } p$.

Calling this $\xi$, we then have
\[
a\left(k + \frac{b}{2a}\right)^{2} + \left(\frac{2b\xi + c}{2a}\right)^{2} = 0, \quad \text{or say}\quad
\left(k + \frac{b}{2a}\right)^{2} + \left(\frac{2b\xi + c}{2a}\right)^{2} = 0,
\]
hence
\[
k = \frac{-b \pm \sqrt{-1}\,(2b\xi + c)}{2a},
\]
where $\xi$ denotes a linear function of $a$ as above; the quadric radical will have a rational value, and the form of the equation thus is
\[
p = \frac{Aa + B}{Ca + D},
\]
where $A$, $B$, $C$, $D$ are rational and integral functions of $p$. But I am not able to carry out the process.

\bigskip\noindent
80.\ As shown, No.\ 78, we have
\[
\frac{p_{2}}{p} = \frac{p^{4} + 5p^{2} + 1}{p^{4} + 29p^{2} + 49}.
\]
Multiplying by the value of $\beta$, ante No.\ 75, we find
\[
\beta = \frac{p^{4} + 29p^{2} + 49}{8p\,(p^{2}+7)},
\]
and we can hence find $A_{1}$ and $A_{2}$ by the formulae
\[
p_{2} = p\,\frac{p^{4} + 5p^{2} + 1}{p^{4} + 29p^{2} + 49}, \qquad
A_{1} = \frac{\beta\,p - p_{2}}{\beta^{2}-1}, \qquad
A_{2} = \frac{\beta\,p_{2} - p}{\beta^{2}-1},
\]
or, for the second of these we may write
\[
A_{2} = \frac{p\,(\beta^{2}-1) - \beta\,(p - \beta\,p_{2})}{\beta^{2}-1} = p - \beta\,A_{1}.
\]
But in a different point of view, regarding only $p_{2}$, but not $p$, as a given function of $p$, $a$, we must to these equations join the equation
\[
12A_{2}^{2} = 6A_{1}A_{2} + 8aA_{1} - p^{4} + 7, \quad \text{ante No.\ 69:}
\]
and we have thus equations for the determination of $A_{1}$, $A_{2}$, and $p$.

\bigskip\noindent
81.\ We have
\begin{multline*}
Q = (p^{4} + 5p^{2} + 1)(p^{4} + 29p^{2} + 49)\,\beta^{2} - 1 \\
= 8p\,(a^{2}+3)\,a\,(p^{4} + 29p^{2} + 49) - 24p\,(p^{2}+7),
\end{multline*}
where the second line is
\[
= a\,(-p^{4} - p^{2} + 7) - 4p\,(p^{2}+7) \cdot \frac{p^{4} + 29p^{2} + 49}{p^{4} + 5p^{2} + 1}.
\]
Uniting the two terms, we have a denominator $8p\,(p^{4}+5p^{2}+1)$, and in the numerator a term $8pa^{2}$ which may be got rid of by means of the $a$-equation; the numerator thus becomes
\begin{multline*}
= 96p\,(-p^{4} - p^{2} + 7) - 128p^{2}\,(p^{2}+7)\,a \\
+ (a^{2}-1)\,\{(-p^{4}-p^{2}+7)\,(p^{8} + 11p^{6} + 63p^{4} + 70p^{2} - 7)\} \\
+ (p^{4} + 5p^{2} + 1)^{2}\,(p^{4} + 13p^{2} + 49) - 32p^{2}\,(p^{2}+7),
\end{multline*}
where the whole divides by $8p$; and we finally obtain
\[
Q = \frac{12\,(-p^{4} - p^{2} + 7) - 16p\,(p^{2}+7)\,a + (a^{2}-1)\,p\,(p^{8} + 17p^{6} + 102p^{4} + 225p^{2} + 97)}{p^{4} + 5p^{2} + 1}.
\]

\bigskip\noindent
82.\ Proceeding to calculate the value of $A_{1}^{2} + A_{2}^{2} + a\,A_{1}A_{2}$, we then have
\[
A_{1}^{2} + A_{2}^{2} + a\,A_{1}A_{2} = \frac{(\beta^{2} - a\beta + 1)\,p_{2}^{2} + (a - 2\beta)\,p\,p_{2} + p^{2}}{\beta^{2} - 1}.
\]
Multiplying the numerator and denominator by $a^{2} + 3$, we have in the numerator a term in $8a^{3}$, which may be got rid of by means of the $pa$-equation; the numerator thus becomes
\begin{multline*}
= (a^{2} + 3)\,(a^{2} - a\beta + 1)\,p_{2}^{2} \\
+ \{(a^{2} + 3)\,a - 16p\,(p^{2}+7)\}\,p\,p_{2} + (a^{2}+3)\,p^{2},
\end{multline*}
which in virtue of
\[
\beta = \frac{p^{4} + 29p^{2} + 49}{8p\,(p^{2}+7)}
\]
is a rational and integral function of $p$, $a$, and $p_{2}$; and the whole expression divides by $a^{2} + 3$.

But
\[
p_{2} = p\,\frac{p^{4} + 5p^{2} + 1}{p^{4} + 29p^{2} + 49},
\]
and substituting this value, the expression becomes a rational and integral function of $p$ and $a$; and writing as before $a^{2} = 3 + 8pa$ (where observe that $a$ satisfies the $a$-equation), the result will be a linear function of $a$.

\bigskip\noindent
83.\ There exists a very elegant formula for the modulus $k$ of the transformed elliptic function. We have
\[
k = \frac{p^{4} + 29p^{2} + 49}{8p\,(p^{2}+7)} \cdot \frac{p^{4} + 5p^{2} + 1}{p^{4} + 29p^{2} + 49} = \frac{p^{4} + 5p^{2} + 1}{8p\,(p^{2}+7)}.
\]
But it is to be remarked that the value of $k$ is not in general expressible as a rational function of $p$ and $a$; it is only when $p$ is expressible as a rational function of $p$ and $a$ that $k$ becomes so expressible.

\bigskip\noindent
84.\ I verify that the modular equation is satisfied for the foregoing value of $k$. The modular equation may be written
\[
\sqrt{k\,k'} + \sqrt{l\,l'} = 1,
\]
where $k$, $k'$ are the complementary moduli; or say
\[
\sqrt{k\,l} + \sqrt{k'\,l'} = 1,
\]
where $k$, $l$ are any two roots of the modular equation, and $k'$, $l'$ are the complementary moduli. Writing $k = \frac{p^{4} + 5p^{2} + 1}{8p\,(p^{2}+7)}$, we have
\[
k' = \frac{\sqrt{(p^{4} + 29p^{2} + 49)^{2} - (p^{4} + 5p^{2} + 1)^{2}}}{8p\,(p^{2}+7)},
\]
and the equation to be verified is
\[
\sqrt{k\,l} + \sqrt{k'\,l'} = 1.
\]

\bigskip\noindent
85.\ With a formula of transformation
\[
y = \frac{x\,(1 + \alpha x^{2} + \beta x^{4})}{1 + \gamma x^{2} + \delta x^{4}},
\]
where $\alpha$, $\beta$, $\gamma$, $\delta$ are given functions of $n$, the order being $= 2\nu + 1$, we have
\[
M = \frac{1}{2\nu+1}\,\frac{y}{x} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \frac{y^{2}}{x^{2}}} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - k^{2}x^{2}y^{2}}
\]
for the multiplier. And thence
\[
M = \frac{1}{2\nu+1}\,\frac{1 + \alpha x^{2} + \beta x^{4}}{1 + \gamma x^{2} + \delta x^{4}} \cdot \frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{x\,(1 + \alpha x^{2} + \beta x^{4})}{1 + \gamma x^{2} + \delta x^{4}}\right) \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \left(\frac{1 + \alpha x^{2} + \beta x^{4}}{1 + \gamma x^{2} + \delta x^{4}}\right)^{2}} \cdot \frac{1}{1 - k^{2}x^{2}\left(\frac{1 + \alpha x^{2} + \beta x^{4}}{1 + \gamma x^{2} + \delta x^{4}}\right)^{2}}.
\]

\newpage
% =====================================================================
% PAPER 871
% =====================================================================
\section[871]{871. A Case of Complex Multiplication with Imaginary Modulus.}
\label{sec:871}

[From the \textit{Proceedings of the London Mathematical Society}, vol.\ xix.\ (1888), pp.\ 480, 481.]

\medskip\noindent
THE case in question is that of the elliptic functions which satisfy the equation
\[
\frac{dy}{\sqrt{1 - y^{4}}} = (1 + \sqrt{-1})\,\frac{dx}{\sqrt{1 - x^{4}}};
\]
so that the multiplier is $M = 1 + \sqrt{-1}$.

Here, writing the modular equation in the form
\[
\sqrt{k\,l} + \sqrt{k'\,l'} = 1,
\]
and observing that $k = l$, we have $k + k' = 1$, that is $k^{2} + k'^{2} = 1$, and thence $k^{2} = k'^{2}$, that is $k = k'$, or say the modulus is $k = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$.

We have for the transformation $n = 1 + \sqrt{-1}$, order $= 2$, and
\[
\sn\,(Mu, k) = \frac{(1 + \sqrt{-1})\,x}{1 + \sqrt{-1}\,x^{2}}, \qquad x = \sn\,(u, k).
\]

Writing $x = \frac{1}{t}$, $y = \frac{1}{s}$, we have $s^{2} = 1 - t^{2}$, and thence $s = \sqrt{1 - t^{2}}$; the formula may be written
\[
\frac{1}{y} = \frac{1}{(1 + \sqrt{-1})\,x} + \frac{\sqrt{-1}\,x}{1 + \sqrt{-1}},
\]
that is
\[
\frac{1}{y} = \frac{1 + \sqrt{-1}\,x^{2}}{(1 + \sqrt{-1})\,x},
\]
or, what is the same thing,
\[
y = \frac{(1 + \sqrt{-1})\,x}{1 + \sqrt{-1}\,x^{2}}.
\]

\newpage
% =====================================================================
% PAPER 872
% =====================================================================
\section[872]{872. On the Finite Number of the Covariants of a Binary Quantic.}
\label{sec:872}

[From the \textit{Mathematische Annalen}, t.\ xxxiv.\ (1889), pp.\ 319--320.]

\medskip\noindent
THE proof of Gordan's theorem given in this paper is insufficient; but the theorem that the number of covariants of a binary quantic is finite is proved in Vol.\ IX.\ paper 320. I here work out a particular case of the proof; viz.\ showing for the quintic that the number of the covariants is finite.

\medskip\noindent
For the quintic $(a, b, c, d, e, f\,\raisebox{-.5ex}{$\hspace{-.2em}\vert\hspace{-.2em}$}\,x, y)^{5}$, the non-unitary irreducible covariants are
\begin{align*}
&(a, b, c, d, e, f\,\raisebox{-.5ex}{$\hspace{-.2em}\vert\hspace{-.2em}$}\,x, y)^{5}, && \text{deg.\ 1, order 5}, \\
&(af - 4be + 3c^{2},\,\ldots\,\raisebox{-.5ex}{$\hspace{-.2em}\vert\hspace{-.2em}$}\,x, y)^{2}, && \text{deg.\ 2, order 2}, \\
&(abc + 2bcd - ade - b^{2}e - c^{2}d,\,\ldots), && \text{deg.\ 3, order 3}, \\
&\text{and } \quad a^{2}d - 3abc + 2b^{3},\,\ldots, && \text{deg.\ 3, order 3},
\end{align*}
where the symbol $(\ldots)$ denotes that the whole series of terms is to be written down; and the number of these covariants is finite.
